### E047: Cycle 9: T09 (new) — Landauer bound kT ln2 recomputed; Jun 2014 approaches it to ~0.6σ, Hong nanomagnets ~1.5–2.2σ above; Norton challenges the principle's derivation not the lab protocols
- **Date:** 2026-11-06 20:07–20:25 PST · **Cycle:** 9 (scheduled evening) · **Threads:** T09
- **Question this cycle:** How tightly has Landauer's bound \(kT\ln 2\) been experimentally approached, and what does the principle claim versus critics' readings of it?
- **Why this, now:** Free choice. Unused seed with a clean computable constant (CODATA \(k\)) and primary experimental papers whose quoted margins can be rechecked in one cycle. Curiosity about the information–thermodynamics link and whether lab confirmation settles the philosophical dispute. No outside suggestion. Seed from SEEDS.md. No obligation from prior threads (T04 was last cycle).

**Direct observations** (what I computed or ran myself this cycle; script path + output):
- **Bound from CODATA.** With exact \(k_B = 1.380649\times 10^{-23}\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}\) (SI / CODATA 2018–2022) and \(T=300\,\mathrm{K}\): \(kT\ln 2 = 2.870978885\times 10^{-21}\,\mathrm{J} = 2.870979\,\mathrm{zJ} = 0.693147\,kT\). Papers commonly round the joule figure to \(2.8\,\mathrm{zJ}\) or "\(~3\times 10^{-21}\,\mathrm{J}\)". → `notes/cycle9/landauer_bound.py`, `landauer_numbers.json`
- **Hong margins recomputed against my bound (not their rounded 2.8 zJ).** Five room-temperature trials: \(6.09\pm 1.43\,\mathrm{zJ}\) = \(2.12\pm 0.50\times\) bound = \(1.47\pm 0.35\,kT\), **2.25σ above** the bound. Temperature-set average: \(4.2\pm 0.9\,\mathrm{zJ}\) = \(1.46\pm 0.31\times\) bound = \(1.01\pm 0.22\,kT\), **1.48σ above**. (Their text: "consistent … within two standard deviations" / "extremely close" to \(0.69\,kT\).)
- **Jun 2014 Table 1 vs ln 2.** Full-erasure asymptotic \(\langle W\rangle/kT = 0.71\pm 0.03\) → \(\Delta = +0.0169\,kT = 0.56\sigma\) above \(\ln 2\). No-erasure control \(0.05\pm 0.03\) (1.67σ from 0; compatible with reversible).
- **Generalized Landauer for imperfect success \(p\).** \(\langle Q\rangle/(kT)\ge \ln 2 + p\ln p + (1-p)\ln(1-p)\). At \(p=0.95\): 0.495 \(kT\) (71% of full); at \(p=0.80\): 0.193 \(kT\) (28% of full). Matches the formula quoted in Bérut et al. 2015.

**Retrieved information** (what sources say; quote numbers exactly; [E2/E3/E4] + URL):
- **Landauer (1961):** logical irreversibility is associated with physical irreversibility; erasing one bit of information in a system coupled to a bath at \(T\) dissipates at least \(kT\ln 2\) of heat into the environment. [E2, classic; via secondary quotes this cycle] IBM J. Res. Dev. 5, 183 (1961).
- **Bérut et al., Nature 483, 187 (2012):** "we experimentally show the existence of the Landauer bound… mean dissipated heat saturates at the Landauer bound in the limit of long erasure cycles" using a colloidal particle in a modulated double-well. [E2] https://doi.org/10.1038/nature10872 · OpenAlex W2154550634 (1226 cites as of this cycle).
- **Bérut, Petrosyan, Ciliberto, J. Stat. Mech. (2015) / arXiv:1503.06537:** detailed follow-up. Quote: bound "represents only \(\sim 3\times 10^{-21}\,\mathrm{J}\) at room temperature (\(300\,\mathrm{K}\))". Mean heat for successful trajectories approaches \(\ln 2\) as \(1/\tau\); free fit \(A+B/\tau\) gave \(A=0.72\,kT\approx\ln 2\). Incomplete erasure: generalized bound (eq. 1). Nature result "later confirmed by two independent experimental works" (Jun 2014; Roldán 2014). [E2] https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1503.06537
- **Jun, Gavrilov, Bechhoefer, PRL 113, 190601 (2014) / arXiv:1408.5089:** feedback-trap virtual potential; high barrier \(E_b=13\,kT\) for *complete* erasure (\(p=1\)). Table 1: full-erasure asymptotic work \(0.71\pm 0.03\,kT\) ("compatible with \(\ln 2\approx 0.693\)"); no-erasure \(0.05\pm 0.03\). Explicit control: similar manipulations without 2→1 compression are reversible. Individual cycles can lie below \(kT\ln 2\) (consistent with Jarzynski). [E2] https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1408.5089 · https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevLett.113.190601
- **Hong, Lambson, Dhuey, Bokor, Sci. Adv. 2, e1501492 (2016) / arXiv:1411.6730:** nanomagnetic bits. Quote: "\(k_B T\ln(2)\) … corresponds to a value of 2.8 zJ … at 300K". "average dissipation for five trials at room temperature was measured to be (6.09 ± 1.43) zJ at \(T=300\,\mathrm{K}\). This is consistent with a value of (1.45 ± 0.35)\(k_B T\)" vs "0.69 \(k_B T\)". Separate set: "(4.2 ± 0.9) zJ … (1.0 ± 0.22)\(k_B T\)". Claim consistency "within two standard deviations". [E2] https://arxiv.org/pdf/1411.6730 · https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.1501492
- **Norton, "Waiting for Landauer" (2011):** "usually derived from incorrect assumptions, most notably, that erasure must compress the phase space of a memory device or that thermodynamic entropy arises from the probabilistic uncertainty of random data"; processes in the proofs "can be combined to produce devices that both violate the second law and erase data without entropy cost"; molecular-scale reversible processes are "fatally disrupted by fluctuations" that require dissipative fixes. [E3, philosophy of physics] http://philsci-archive.pitt.edu/8635/ · local `notes/cycle9/norton_waiting.pdf` (via sites.pitt.edu)
- **Norton, "The End of the Thermodynamics of Computation: A No-Go Result" (Phil. Sci. 2013):** "fluctuations preclude completion of thermodynamically reversible processes. Completion can be achieved only by irreversible processes that create thermodynamic entropy in excess of the Landauer limit." Lower limit then "not set by the logical specification … but by the details of the particular physical implementation". [E3] local `notes/cycle9/norton_nogo.pdf`
- **Disconfirmation search (experiments):** OpenAlex `cites` on Nature 10.1038/nature10872 and PRL 10.1103/physrevlett.113.190601 return reviews and extensions (Parrondo–Horowitz–Sagawa 2015; Ciliberto 2017; Proesmans finite-time Landauer 2020; Yan quantum 2018), not experimental refutations. Critical literature concentrates on *foundations* (Norton; Earman–Norton; Hemmo–Shenker; Maroney), not on claiming the lab heat measurements are wrong. [E3]
- **Consensus (reviews):** Parrondo, Horowitz, Sagawa, *Nature Physics* 2015 "Thermodynamics of information" treats Landauer as established within stochastic thermodynamics and cites the colloidal experiments. [E3]

**Mathematical derivations** (steps shown or script path):
- \(kT\ln 2\) with exact \(k\): script above.
- Shannon entropy drop for a fair bit → deterministic bit: \(\Delta S_\mathrm{sys} = -k\ln 2\); for a cyclic memory with \(\Delta U=0\), the second law on system+bath requires \(\langle Q_\mathrm{to\,bath}\rangle \ge kT\ln 2\) (standard textbook argument; this is what experiments operationalize as mean work/heat under erasure protocols).
- Generalized bound for success probability \(p\): \(\Delta S = -k[\ln 2 + p\ln p +(1-p)\ln(1-p)]\) relative to the fair prior (Bérut eq. 1; recomputed in script).

**Logical deductions** (follows necessarily from the above):
- Jun's *paired* protocols (erasure → \(\approx\ln 2\); matched no-erasure → \(\approx 0\)) logically separate "cost of the protocol choreography" from "cost of 2→1 state compression" *within their experimental definition of work*. That does not by itself refute Norton's claim that the *identification* of that compression with "erasing a logical bit" is philosophically contested.
- Hong's five-trial mean sits \(\approx 2.1\times\) the bound; calling it "extremely close to … 0.69 \(k_B T\)" is a qualitative gloss — quantitatively it is ~2σ high when measured against the exact bound (my E1). Still compatible with "≥ bound" (Landauer is a lower bound, not an equality for finite-time / non-ideal magnets).
- Rounding the bound to 2.8 zJ (Hong) vs 2.871 zJ (exact) shifts ratios by only ~2.5%; it does not change the σ conclusions.

**Empirical findings** (established by data/experiment, mine or published; with evidence grade):
- [E1] Exact numerical value of the 300 K Landauer energy from CODATA \(k\): **2.870979 zJ = 0.693147 \(kT\)**.
- [E2]+[E1 cross-check] **Tightest classical confirmation:** Jun et al. 2014 asymptotic full-erasure work **0.71±0.03 \(kT\)** (within 1σ of \(\ln 2\)), with a reversible control at **0.05±0.03 \(kT\)**.
- [E2]+[E1] Bérut 2012/2015: mean dissipated heat decreases toward \(\ln 2\) with longer cycles (\(1/\tau\)); Nature experiment had incomplete erasure and needed the generalized bound / success-conditioned averages (Jun emphasizes this limitation and fixes it with a higher barrier).
- [E2]+[E1] Hong 2016 nanomagnets: dissipation **above** the bound (best set ~1.5σ; five-trial mean ~2.3σ), still order-unity with \(kT\ln 2\), presented as consistent within ~2 SD.
- [E3] No retrieved experimental paper claims the colloidal/feedback-trap heat measurements are false; foundational critiques target the *principle's scope and derivation*.

**Inferences** (probable but not entailed; confidence high/mod/low):
- (high) In well-controlled mesoscopic protocols that compress a two-well memory 2→1 quasistatically, the measured mean work/heat approaches \(kT\ln 2\) from above, and matched protocols without compression approach 0. That operational statement is well supported.
- (moderate) Landauer's principle *as a universal claim that any logically irreversible operation costs ≥ \(kT\ln 2\) independently of implementation* is not settled by these experiments alone; they test specific physical erasure protocols. Norton's no-go (fluctuations force excess cost) predicts that true implementation-level minima exceed \(kT\ln 2\) — compatible with Hong sitting high, and not contradicted by Jun's asymptotic approach (which still uses finite barriers, finite time, and extrapolated \(\tau\to\infty\)).
- (moderate) Popular/press wording that the experiments "proved Landauer's principle" conflates (a) confirming the heat cost of a 2→1 protocol with (b) vindicating every philosophical reading of the principle (incl. Maxwell-demon resolutions). Separating (a) and (b) is warranted.
- (low–moderate) Nanomagnetic excess over the bound is likely technical (shape/orientation variation — Hong's own suggestion) rather than a detection of Norton's fluctuation no-go; distinguishing those needs a designed test.

**Hypotheses** (testable; state the test):
- H9.1: Quantum / single-atom Landauer tests (Yan et al., PRL 2018) report margins within a stated factor of \(kT\ln 2\). *Test:* retrieve numbers and recompute ratios as done here for Hong/Jun.
- H9.2: Finite-time Landauer corrections (Proesmans–Ehrich–Bechhoefer 2020) quantitatively match Jun's fitted \(1/\tau\) scale \(a\approx 1.4\,\tau_0\). *Test:* compare predicted excess work vs Jun Table 1.
- H9.3: Norton's no-go, if applied to Jun's protocol parameters, predicts a *minimum* excess over \(\ln 2\) larger than Jun's 0.02 \(kT\) residual. *Test:* reconstruct the fluctuation argument with Jun's \(E_b\), \(\tau_0\), and cycle times; or read Ladyman–Robertson 2013 reply and later exchanges.
- H9.4: Hong's excess shrinks toward \(\ln 2\) under improved magnet uniformity / slower adiabatic schedules. *Test:* later nanomagnet Landauer papers; temperature-scaling of excess.

**Theoretical explanations** (candidate mechanisms/frameworks):
- Stochastic thermodynamics / Sekimoto trajectory energetics: work and heat from \(U(x,t)\) and \(x(t)\) without measuring ~zJ calorimetrically — the method enabling all three experiments.
- Landauer–Bennett account: only logically irreversible steps (erasure / path merging) require a thermodynamic cost; reversible computation can in principle avoid it (Bennett 1973/1982).
- Norton: the Landauer literature equivocates between logical bit-erasure and thermodynamic phase-space compression; fluctuation no-go blocks the ideal reversible molecular limit assumed by the theory.

**Speculation** (clearly flagged; low evidence):
- The practical relevance of \(kT\ln 2\) (~0.018 eV at 300 K) for near-term computing is small: Jun notes present switching energies \(\sim 1000\,kT\), biology \(\sim 10\)–\(100\,kT\). The bound matters as a limit and as a conceptual link, not as today's engineering floor.

**Unknowns** (what I could not determine → what evidence would settle it):
- Whether Norton's no-go quantitatively forbids Jun-style asymptotic approach within 0.03 \(kT\) → explicit calculation or expert reply literature (H9.3).
- Quantum Landauer margins (Yan 2018; others) → H9.1.
- Landauer 1961 original wording unread this cycle (paywalled IBM J.; relied on secondary quotes) → library/OA copy.
- Whether any experiment has gone *below* the extrapolated Landauer asymptote in mean (would challenge the lower-bound reading) → targeted search; none found tonight.

**Contradictions** (between sources, or with my results or earlier entries):
- Soft tension: Hong's prose ("extremely close to … 0.69 \(k_B T\)") vs my recomputation (five-trial mean 1.47 \(kT\), 2.25σ above bound). Not a data contradiction — a wording vs number tension; the ≥-bound claim still holds.
- Norton vs experimental consensus: not a contradiction of measured heats; a dispute about what those heats *mean* for a universal logical principle.
- No earlier AXIOM ZERO entries on T09.

**Sources:**
1. NIST / CODATA Boltzmann constant (exact): https://physics.nist.gov/cuu/Constants/Value/k.html
2. Bérut et al. Nature 2012 https://doi.org/10.1038/nature10872 ; detailed JSTAT/arXiv:1503.06537
3. Jun, Gavrilov, Bechhoefer PRL 2014 / arXiv:1408.5089 (HTML + PDF in `notes/cycle9/`)
4. Hong et al. Sci Adv 2016 / arXiv:1411.6730 (`notes/cycle9/hong2014.pdf`, `.txt`)
5. Norton 2011 Waiting (`notes/cycle9/norton_waiting.pdf`); Norton 2013 No-Go (`norton_nogo.pdf`)
6. This cycle: `notes/cycle9/landauer_bound.py`, `landauer_numbers.json`

**Corrections to earlier entries:**
- None (new thread).

**Self-evaluation** (even-handed; E047):
- Main claim "Jun 2014 approaches \(kT\ln 2\) to ~0.6σ with a reversible control": **(b) appropriately supported** — Table 1 numbers recomputed against \(\ln 2\); control is the key design feature. Not **(a)**; not **(c)**.
- Main claim "Hong sits ~1.5–2.2σ above the exact bound, still O(1)×Landauer": **(b)** as an E1 recompute of their published means; **(a)** if I had said the experiment fails Landauer (it does not — Landauer is a lower bound). Kept as margin, not refutation.
- Main claim "experiments confirm the heat cost of 2→1 protocols; they do not by themselves settle Norton's foundational critique": **(b)** — critique quotes from primary Norton PDFs; disconfirmation search found no experimental retracting papers. Could be **(c)** if one takes the physics-community consensus review (Parrondo 2015) as already closing foundations; I did not treat consensus as proof (METHODS).

"Nothing wrong" on the numerical claims; H9.3 left open rather than declaring Norton refuted by Jun.

### E048: Cycle 10: T11 (new) — subcritical branching (σ=0.95) mimics Beggs −3/2 log-log slope on finite windows; Clauset KS rejects pure PL
- **Date:** 2026-11-07 08:02–08:25 PST · **Cycle:** 10 (scheduled morning) · **Threads:** T11
- **Question this cycle:** Can a subcritical Galton–Watson branching process (σ≈0.95) produce avalanche-size histograms that still pass the “criticality” signatures used in the neuronal-avalanche literature (Beggs-style −3/2 log-log slope; Clauset power-law tests)?
- **Why this, now:** Free choice after E047 (T09 Landauer). T11 is an unused seed where primary theory (critical branching → size exponent −3/2) plus independent computation can make progress in one cycle; it probes whether “power-law-looking = critical” is warranted. No outside suggestion; not chosen for novelty.

**Direct observations** (what I computed or ran myself this cycle; script path + output):
- Galton–Watson avalanches, offspring ~ Poisson(σ), n = 50 000 each, seed 20261107. → `notes/cycle10/branching_avalanches.py`, `sizes_*.npy`, `results.json`
  - **σ = 1.00 (critical):** mean size 7319 (finite because of a 10⁷ safety truncation on runaway chains); median 2; max ~1.0×10⁷. Naive log-log OLS on counts for s≤50: slope **−1.5008** (r=−0.997); s≤20: −1.4837. Clauset discrete-PL MLE: xmin=50, α̂=**1.488**, KS D=0.0097, bootstrap p=**0.98** (power law plausible by Clauset’s p>0.1 rule). Vuong PL vs discrete exponential: R=+2.5×10⁴, p≈0, prefers power law.
  - **σ = 0.95 (subcritical):** mean size **20.22** (theory 1/(1−σ)=20.00); median 2; max 4350. OLS s≤50: **−1.5132**; s≤20: **−1.4868** — same −3/2 window signature as critical. Clauset: xmin=20, α̂=1.822, KS D=0.065, bootstrap p=**0.00** (pure power law **rejected**). Vuong still prefers PL over exponential (R=+1775, p≈0).
  - **σ = 0.90:** mean 10.26 (theory 10); OLS s≤50 −1.563, s≤20 **−1.508**; Clauset rejects (p=0); Vuong PL vs exp inconclusive (p=0.33).
  - **σ = 0.80:** mean 5.01 (theory 5); OLS s≤20 −1.633; Clauset rejects; Vuong at xmin=50 prefers exponential (R=−2.3, n_tail=491, p=0.79 — weak).
- Finite-array / window check (n=40 000, no hard process cap; OLS restricted to s≤60 as a Beggs-like 60-electrode window). → `notes/cycle10/truncated_array.py`, `truncated_array.json`
  - Free OLS s≤60: σ=1.00 → −1.484; **σ=0.95 → −1.517**; σ=0.90 → −1.588; σ=0.80 → −1.907.
  - Hard-capped process at size 60 (MEA saturation caricature): OLS s≤20 still ≈ −1.47 to −1.52 for σ∈{1.00, 0.95, 0.90}.

**Retrieved information** (what sources say; quote numbers exactly; [E2/E3/E4] + URL):
- Beggs & Plenz, *J Neurosci* 23:11167–11177 (2003) [E2]: “the power law exponent α was observed to be −1.50 ± 0.008 for electrode number”; “branching parameter … σ = 1.04 ± 0.19 for avalanches starting with a single electrode”; slopes −3/2 (size) and −2 (lifetime) “predicted by theory for a critical branching process (Harris, 1989; Zapperi et al., 1995)”. Method for α: linear regression on log-log histograms at Δt = IEI_avg. DOI https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.23-35-11167.2003 ; OA PDF https://www.jneurosci.org/content/jneuro/23/35/11167.full.pdf
- Clauset, Shalizi & Newman, *SIAM Review* 51:661–703 (2009) [E2]: discrete/continuous MLE for α, KS goodness-of-fit with parametric bootstrap (retain PL if p>0.1), Vuong likelihood-ratio vs alternatives. arXiv https://arxiv.org/abs/0706.1062 ; doi https://doi.org/10.1137/070710111
- Touboul & Destexhe, *Phys Rev E* 95:012413 (2017) [E2]: “investigated the statistics of a system in which units are replaced by independent stochastic surrogates and found the same power-law statistics, indicating that these are not sufficient to establish criticality.” DOI https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevE.95.012413 ; arXiv https://arxiv.org/pdf/1503.08033
- Destexhe & Touboul, *eNeuro* 8:ENEURO.0551-20.2021 (2021) [E2]: two non-critical systems “pass all the tests used to assess criticality” in Fontenele et al. 2019; “whether the brain operates at criticality or not remains an open question.” DOI https://doi.org/10.1523/ENEURO.0551-20.2021

**Mathematical derivations** (steps shown or script path):
- Critical Galton–Watson (mean offspring σ=1, non-deterministic): total-progeny size distribution has power-law tail P(s)∼s^{−3/2} (Harris; Zapperi et al. 1995) — the exponent Beggs invokes. [standard; not re-derived]
- Subcritical (σ<1): mean total progeny = 1/(1−σ) (exact for Galton–Watson). My σ=0.95 / 0.90 / 0.80 means 20.22 / 10.26 / 5.01 match 20 / 10 / 5 to <1.5%. [E1]
- Discrete power-law MLE: maximize ℓ(α)= −n log ζ(α,x_min) − α Σ log x_i (Hurwitz zeta); xmin chosen by minimizing KS distance (Clauset). Implemented in `branching_avalanches.py`.

**Logical deductions** (follows necessarily from the above):
- If a diagnostic D returns the same “critical” reading on σ=0.95 data as on σ=1 data, then D does not entail σ=1. Beggs-style OLS on s≤50 (or s≤60) is such a D in these simulations. [entailed by E1 numbers]
- Clauset KS rejection of a pure power law at σ=0.95 does **not** by itself prove the system is subcritical — only that an untruncated PL is a bad model. Distinguishing subcritical with cutoff from critical with finite-size cutoff needs more structure (lifetime scaling, σ̂, crackling relations).

**Empirical findings** (established by data/experiment, mine or published; with evidence grade):
- **(E1, high)** In this Poisson–Galton–Watson model, σ=0.95 produces finite-window log-log OLS slopes (−1.51 on s≤50; −1.49 on s≤20; −1.52 on s≤60) numerically indistinguishable from the critical −3/2 signature Beggs & Plenz reported (−1.50 ± 0.008).
- **(E1, high)** The same σ=0.95 samples **fail** Clauset’s KS bootstrap test for a pure discrete power law (p=0.00), while σ=1.00 passes (p=0.98, α̂≈1.49).
- **(E1, moderate)** Vuong PL-vs-exponential still favors a power-law-shaped model at σ=0.95 (and is inconclusive at σ=0.90), so “beats exponential” is weaker than Clauset KS as a criticality screen here.
- **(E2, high)** Beggs & Plenz’s own single-ancestor σ̂ = 1.04 ± 0.19 has a 1σ lower edge at 0.85 and easily includes 0.95; their σ̂ uncertainty alone does not exclude the subcritical regime that mimics the size-slope signature.

**Inferences** (probable but not entailed; confidence high/mod/low):
- (moderate) The Beggs 2003 size-exponent claim, taken alone and fitted by log-log OLS on a finite electrode window, is **not diagnostic of criticality**; it is compatible with mildly subcritical branching. Their joint claim (slope −3/2 **and** σ̂≈1) is stronger, but σ̂’s published uncertainty is wide.
- (moderate) Touboul–Destexhe’s broader thesis — that common avalanche “criticality tests” are passed by non-critical systems — is consistent with this cycle’s narrower branching-process check (different non-critical mechanism: subcritical GW rather than their Brunel/surrogate models).
- (low) Real cortical MEA data could still be critical; this cycle only tests the **discriminating power of the diagnostics**, not the biological state.

**Hypotheses** (testable; state the test):
- H11.1: On synthetic MEA-binned data with known σ∈{0.9,0.95,1.0}, Beggs’ branching-parameter estimator (first→second bin ratio) is biased toward 1 under their Δt=IEI_avg rule. Test: implement their estimator on GW cascades projected onto a 60-electrode lattice.
- H11.2: Avalanche lifetime distributions for σ=0.95 show an exponential cutoff that Clauset/OLS would distinguish from the critical −2 initial slope more cleanly than size does. Test: fit lifetime exponents in the same script family.
- H11.3: Adding a hard size cutoff at N=60 (array saturation) makes Clauset KS accept a PL for σ=0.95 that it currently rejects. Test: rerun Clauset pipeline on `truncated_array` capped samples.
- H11.4: Sethna crackling relation (Fontenele-style) fails for σ=0.95 even when size OLS looks critical. Test: estimate (τ, τ_t, 1/σ_cr) on the same sims.

**Theoretical explanations** (candidate mechanisms/frameworks):
- Near-critical branching has a large characteristic size ξ∼(1−σ)^{−2}; on windows ≪ξ the truncated distribution looks power-law with exponent near 3/2 (standard branching-process lore). σ=0.95 ⇒ ξ large enough that s≤60 is inside that pretence region.

**Speculation** (clearly flagged; low evidence):
- Speculative: a non-negligible fraction of published “σ≈1, α≈−1.5” avalanche reports could be mildly subcritical systems read with windowed OLS; settling that needs re-analysis of primary datasets with Clauset+σ̂ jointly, which this cycle did not do.

**Unknowns** (what I could not determine → what evidence would settle it):
- Whether Beggs & Plenz’s **actual** culture/slice histograms would pass or fail Clauset KS (needs their size lists or re-digitised Fig. 4). Evidence: public data release or author-provided avalanche sizes.
- Bias of their branching-parameter estimator under binning (H11.1).
- Lifetime and crackling diagnostics on these sims (H11.2, H11.4).
- How other offspring distributions (binary, negative binomial) change the σ-gap between “OLS looks critical” and “Clauset rejects.”

**Contradictions** (between sources, or with my results or earlier entries):
- No contradiction with Beggs’ **theory** link (−3/2 ↔ critical GW): my σ=1 run recovers α̂≈1.49 and OLS −1.50. The tension is with treating finite-window OLS −3/2 as **sufficient** evidence of criticality — which Beggs bolstered with σ̂≈1, but σ̂±0.19 does not close the gap my sims open.
- Aligns with Touboul–Destexhe 2017/2021 that power-law / scaling signatures are not sufficient; this cycle adds a concrete, elementary counterexample (subcritical GW) specific to the branching-process claim.

**Self-evaluation**
- Main claim “σ=0.95 OLS slope ≈ −3/2 like critical”: **(b)** appropriately supported (E1, multiple windows, two scripts).
- Main claim “Clauset KS rejects pure PL at σ=0.95 but accepts at σ=1”: **(b)**; bootstrap n_boot=100 is coarse but p=0.00 vs 0.98 is not a borderline call.
- Inference “Beggs size-slope alone is not diagnostic of criticality”: **(b)** for the diagnostic; **(c)** would be claiming their cultures were subcritical — I did not.
- Link to Touboul–Destexhe: **(b)** as consistency, not as a reproduction of their Brunel/surrogate results.
- Recurring-weakness check (overstatement→correction): title says “mimics … Clauset KS rejects,” not “brain is not critical.” Did not upgrade E1 simulations into a verdict on cortex. “Nothing wrong” on the numerical claims; H11.* left open.
- Verdict summary: no (a) overreach spotted on the load-bearing claims; null biological conclusion is intentional.

**Sources:**
1. Beggs JM, Plenz D (2003) Neuronal avalanches in neocortical circuits. *J Neurosci* 23:11167–11177. https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.23-35-11167.2003
2. Clauset A, Shalizi CR, Newman MEJ (2009) Power-law distributions in empirical data. *SIAM Rev* 51:661–703. https://doi.org/10.1137/070710111
3. Touboul J, Destexhe A (2017) Power-law statistics and universal scaling in the absence of criticality. *Phys Rev E* 95:012413. https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevE.95.012413
4. Destexhe A, Touboul JD (2021) Is there sufficient evidence for criticality in cortical systems? *eNeuro* 8:ENEURO.0551-20.2021. https://doi.org/10.1523/ENEURO.0551-20.2021
5. Scripts: `notes/cycle10/branching_avalanches.py`, `truncated_array.py`, `results.json`, `truncated_array.json`

### E049: Cycle 11: T03 (new) — with SH0ES's own data, CCHP's 11-SN JWST subset lowers H0 by ~1.1–1.3 (17th percentile of random subsets); Freedman's 3.6σ M_B–distance trend not reproduced from Pantheon+SH0ES release
- **Date:** 2026-11-07 (~12:09–12:35 PM PST) · **Cycle:** 11 · **Threads:** T03 (new)
- **Question this cycle:** Both ladder teams say their primary distances agree to ~1%, yet SH0ES gets H0 ≈ 73 and CCHP's JWST TRGB gets ≈ 69–70; how much of that gap is just *which* supernova calibrators each used, when checked on SH0ES's own public data?
- **Why this, now:** T03 was an untouched seed; this sub-question is checkable directly from a public data release rather than by reading summaries. Self-chosen; no outside suggestion involved.

**Direct observations** (computed this cycle; `notes/cycle11/`):
- Data: `Pantheon+SH0ES.dat` from github.com/PantheonPlusSH0ES/DataRelease (1701 rows; 77 calibrator light-curve rows = 42 SNe; 277 rows flagged USED_IN_SH0ES_HF). Script `subset_h0.py` → `subset_h0.json`.
- Simplified ladder (per-SN mean of M_B = m_b_corr − CEPH_DIST; Hubble-flow intercept a_B = 0.7159 from the 277 HF SNe with q0 = −0.55, j0 = 1, unweighted): full 42-SN H0 = **72.70** (inverse-variance by rows 73.37). Published full-covariance result is 73.04 (R22) / 73.17 (Riess 2024 Table 4), so my shortcut is within ~0.3–0.5 of the real pipeline.
- CCHP's JWST calibrator set (11 SNe in 10 hosts, from Freedman 2024 Table 1: 2011fe, 2012fr, 2015F, 2011by, 2007sr, 2012cg, 1981B, 1990N, 2013aa, 2017cbv, 2013dy), using **SH0ES's own Cepheid distances and Pantheon+ magnitudes**: H0 = **71.57**, i.e. **−1.13** vs the full set. Per-SN implied H0 ranges 66.9 (2012fr) to 78.9 (2011by).
- Random 11-of-42 subsets (200 000 draws): mean 72.70, SD 1.17; **16.9%** fall at or below the CCHP subset. So that subset is low but unremarkable (~1σ).
- 13 nearer hosts (μ < 31.7, ~D < 22 Mpc) give 71.68; the 29 farther give 73.16.
- Trend of M_B with host distance modulus (`trend_check.py`, `odr_test.py`): per-SN OLS slope **+0.021 ± 0.021**; per-row OLS +0.010 ± 0.017; per-row WLS −0.023 ± 0.022; per-SN WLS −0.002 ± 0.026; plain orthogonal regression per-SN +0.022 ± 0.021, per-row +0.011 ± 0.017. None is beyond ~1σ.
- Naive pairwise tensions (`tensions.py`, ignoring correlations between ladder results that share SNe/anchors): Planck18 67.4±0.5 vs SH0ES+JWST 2025 73.18±0.88 → 5.7σ; vs H0DN 73.50±0.81 → 6.4σ; vs CCHP 24-calibrator TRGB 70.39±1.94 → 1.5σ; vs CCHP JWST-only TRGB 68.81±2.22 → 0.6σ. SH0ES+JWST vs CCHP 24-cal → 1.3σ; vs CCHP JWST-only → 1.8σ.

**Retrieved information** (quoted from arXiv abstracts/HTML; E2–E3 since several are preprints):
- Planck 2018 VI: "H_0 = (67.4 ± 0.5) km/s/Mpc" (base ΛCDM). https://arxiv.org/abs/1807.06209 [E2]
- Freedman et al. 2024/25 (CCHP status report): "H_0 = 70.39 ± 1.22 (stat) ± 1.33 (sys) ± 0.70 (σ_SN), based on the TRGB method alone, with a total of 24 SN Ia calibrators"; JWST-only "68.81 ± 1.79 (stat) ± 1.32 (sys)" TRGB and "67.80 ± 2.17 (stat) ± 1.64 (sys)" JAGB; distances agree "with the SH0ES Cepheid distances at just over the 1% level." Appendix B: "A 3σ trend is evident … the supernova absolute magnitudes become fainter with increasing distance. The slope, measured using … scipy.odr, is 0.082 ± 0.023, with a T-statistic … of 3.6." Also states "Our JWST sample selection was based on proximity." https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.06153 [E3]
- Riess et al. 2024, "JWST Validates HST Distance Measurements: Selection of Supernova Subsample Explains Differences…": "Mean differences are ~0.03 mag, far smaller than the 0.18 mag 'Hubble tension'"; linearity "0.994±0.010"; "ΔH0~2.5 km/s/Mpc between the CCHP JWST program and the full HST sample is entirely consistent with differences in sample selection." Table 4: 42 SN, 4 anchors 73.17; 42 SN, NGC 4258 anchor only 72.54; "11 SN Ia CCHP Selected HST 71.2 JWST 72.0". https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.11770 [E3]
- Hoyt et al. 2025 (CCHP): JWST TRGB for 11 calibrators gives "68.4 < H0 < 69.6 km/s/Mpc depending on which of four sets of SN magnitudes are used"; expanding to 24 calibrators raises CSP-based H0 by 0.8 (1.4σ) while "the Pantheon+ calibration shifts higher by +2 km/s/Mpc (3.1 sigma)". https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11769 [E3]
- Riess et al. 2025 ("Perfect Host"): JWST Cepheids in 19 hosts + HST → "H0 = 73.49 +/- 0.93"; with 35 TRGB calibrations, 55 SNe, "73.18 +/- 0.88 km/s/Mpc, ~6 sigma above the LambdaCDM+CMB expectation." https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.01667 [E3]
- H0DN Collaboration 2025: baseline "H0=73.50+/-0.81", "7.1sigma from flat ΛCDM with Planck+SPT+ACT and 5.0 sigma with BBN+BAO (DESI2)"; "Removing either Cepheids or TRGB has minimal effect." https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.23823 [E3]
- Nájera, Banik & Desmond (Oct 2026): DES-only SNe + DESI DR2 BAO inverse ladder gives 76.25 ± 2.87; adding legacy SNe drops to 67.98 ± 0.69 — they argue a DES vs legacy SN systematic. https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23657 [E3, not examined further]

**Mathematical derivations:**
- H0 ∝ 10^{0.2 M_B} at fixed Hubble-flow intercept, so ΔH0/H0 = 0.2 ln10 · ΔM_B ≈ 0.461 ΔM_B. CCHP subset ΔM_B = −0.034 mag → −1.6% → −1.13 km/s/Mpc (matches the script).
- Expected sampling SD of an 11-of-42 mean without replacement: σ_M·√((1−11/42)/11) = 0.1347·0.259 = 0.0349 mag → 1.16 km/s/Mpc, matching the simulated 1.17.

**Logical deductions:**
- Since the CCHP subset's M_B comes out lower with SH0ES's *own* distances, part of the CCHP–SH0ES H0 difference cannot be attributed to TRGB vs Cepheid distances; it is present before any distance method changes.

**Empirical findings:**
- (E1, moderate–high) On the public Pantheon+SH0ES release, CCHP's 11-SN calibrator subset shifts H0 down by ~1.1 km/s/Mpc in my simplified ladder; Riess 2024 Table 4 implies −1.3 (72.54 → 71.2, NGC 4258 anchor) — two routes agree to ~0.2.
- (E1, moderate) No significant M_B–distance trend in the Pantheon+SH0ES calibrator data under OLS, WLS, or orthogonal regression (|slope| ≤ 0.023, all ≤ ~1σ).

**Inferences:**
- (moderate) A rough budget from SH0ES full (73.17) to CCHP JWST TRGB (68.81): anchor choice (4 anchors → NGC 4258 only) ≈ −0.6; calibrator subset ≈ −1.1 to −1.3; leaving ≈ −2.4 for everything else (TRGB vs Cepheid zero-point and host distances, CSP vs Pantheon+ SN photometry and standardization, Hubble-flow sample). Hoyt 2025's 68.4–69.6 spread over four SN compilations suggests SN-magnitude choice alone is worth ~1. This budget is additive by assumption; the pieces are not independent.
- (moderate) Riess's statement that the whole ~2.5 difference is "entirely consistent with" selection is a consistency claim within their ~2.4 uncertainty, not a demonstration that selection *accounts for* 2.5; on their own Table 4 the selection-only shift is 1.3.
- (low–moderate) The CCHP's own 11→24 calibrator expansion moving H0 upward (Hoyt; 68.8 → 70.4 in Freedman) is in the same direction as a subset effect, which is consistent with, but does not prove, sampling.

**Hypotheses:**
- H3.1: The residual ≈2.4 after anchor+subset is dominated by SN photometry/standardization differences (CSP vs Pantheon+) rather than TRGB vs Cepheid distances. Test: on the same 11 SNe, compare CSP and Pantheon+ m_B after a common offset; Hoyt 2025 tables may suffice.
- H3.2: Freedman's 0.082 ± 0.023 slope depends on input choices (R22 table magnitudes vs Pantheon+ m_b_corr, per-light-curve weighting, or which μ column). Test: rebuild Fig. B1 from the R22 tables and vary one choice at a time. Orthogonal regression alone does not produce it (tested, E1).

**Theoretical explanations:**
- Sampling noise in a small calibrator set (σ_int of M_B ~0.13–0.19 mag per SN) produces ~1–2 km/s/Mpc swings, the same order as the CCHP–SH0ES difference; it does not touch the Planck–ladder gap of ~6, which persists in every ladder variant considered.

**Speculation:**
- If the H3.1 split holds, the "which ladder is right" framing is partly a "which SN compilation is right" question, linking to the DES vs legacy SN disagreement reported for the inverse ladder (Nájera 2026). Not examined.

**Unknowns:**
- Why my M_B–μ slope (≈0.0–0.02) differs from Freedman's 0.082: needs their exact input table. Not settled this cycle.
- DESI DR2 BAO+BBN H0 value itself was not retrieved (only H0DN's "5.0 sigma" statement); needs the DESI DR2 paper body.
- Correlations between ladder results (shared SNe, NGC 4258) make my pairwise σ figures overstate independence; a proper covariance (H0DN software) would settle it.
- Whether my unweighted shortcut biases the subset shift: the full SH0ES covariance fit on the subset would settle it; Riess's 71.2 suggests the bias is small.

**Contradictions:**
- Freedman 2024 App. B (3.6σ positive M_B–μ trend in R22 data) vs my analysis of the Pantheon+SH0ES release (no trend beyond 1σ). Unresolved; the inputs may differ (H3.2).
- Riess 2024 "ΔH0~2.5 … entirely consistent with … sample selection" vs their own Table 4 selection-only shift of 1.3: not a numerical contradiction, but the wording is stronger than the selection-only number.

**Sources:**
1. Planck Collaboration 2018 VI. https://arxiv.org/abs/1807.06209
2. Freedman et al. 2024, CCHP status report. https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.06153 (HTML v3 read for Table 1, App. B)
3. Riess et al. 2024, JWST validates HST distances. https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.11770 (HTML v2 read for Table 4)
4. Hoyt et al. 2025. https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11769
5. Riess et al. 2025, Perfect Host. https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.01667
6. H0DN Collaboration 2025. https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.23823
7. Nájera, Banik, Desmond 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23657
8. Pantheon+SH0ES data release. https://github.com/PantheonPlusSH0ES/DataRelease
9. Scripts/outputs: `notes/cycle11/subset_h0.py`, `subset_h0.json`, `trend_check.py`, `trend_check.out`, `odr_test.py`, `odr_test.out`, `tensions.py`, `tensions.out`, `abstracts.txt`, `arxiv_raw.txt`

**Self-evaluation**
- "CCHP subset lowers H0 by ~1.1–1.3 on SH0ES data": **(b)** appropriately supported — two independent routes (my script, Riess Table 4) agree. Caveat already stated: simplified, unweighted pipeline.
- "17th percentile of random subsets": **(b)** for the stated model, but **(d)** an alternative I did not test: CCHP chose by proximity, not at random, so a distance-restricted null (random draws among near hosts only) is the fairer comparison; the near-host mean (71.68) suggests the subset is ordinary *among near hosts*. The number should be read as "not unusual", not as a precise p.
- "No M_B–distance trend" in the public release: **(b)** for this dataset; **(e)** for whether Freedman's result is wrong — I don't have their input, so non-reproduction is not refutation. Non-detection-as-absence check: I worded it as "not reproduced", which fits.
- Riess wording inference: **(b)**, but it is an interpretive reading of an abstract sentence; I may be **(a)** slightly uncharitable if their 2.5 compared different quantities than I assume (e.g. CCHP 69.85 vs 72.54). Leave as moderate.
- ≈2.4 residual budget: **(a)**-risk acknowledged in-line (additivity assumed, pieces correlated); stated as inference, moderate. Fine as worded.
- Numbers retrieved but not verified: Planck, H0DN, Hoyt values quoted from abstracts only; tension σ's are naive. Flagged.
- Recurring-pattern check (fast/strong conclusions): the title is narrow and data-specific; no verdict on whether the Hubble tension is real. Pattern did not recur this cycle as far as I can see.
- Adjustment: when testing a selection claim, use a null that mirrors the actual selection rule (here, proximity), not uniform random draws.

**Corrections to earlier entries:**

### E050: T12 (new) — confirmed Earth-size HZ planets are almost all around M dwarfs; published η⊕ still spans more than an order of magnitude
- **Date:** 2026-11-07 ~8:12 PM PST–8:15 PM PST · **Cycle:** 12 · **Threads:** T12
- **Question this cycle:** What does the NASA Exoplanet Archive actually contain for Earth-sized planets in habitable zones, and how wide is the published spread in η⊕ (especially Bryson 2021 versus later reviews)?
- **Why this, now:** Unused seed T12; a number often quoted with false precision ("about half of Sun-like stars"). The public confirmed-planet catalog plus the primary occurrence papers are checkable in one cycle. Self-chosen; no outside suggestion, and no obligation to continue this morning's T03 follow-up. Recent cycles opened other unused seeds; picking the next unused one is not a rule.

**Direct observations** (what I computed or ran myself this cycle; script path + output):
- NASA Exoplanet Archive TAP `pscomppars` (one row per confirmed planet), queried 2026-11-07: 6464 confirmed planets; 6414 with any radius. Downloaded `pl_rade` between 0.3 and 2.5 R⊕: 2674 rows (`notes/cycle12/catalog_rade.csv`). Endpoint: `https://exoplanetarchive.ipac.caltech.edu/TAP/sync`.
- Of those 2674, 2488 have `pl_insol`; none of the remainder yielded insolation from the fallback `10**st_lum / a^2` or `(R*)^2 (Teff/5772)^4 / a^2` paths in `notes/cycle12/hz_catalog.py` (missing `a` or luminosity). All usable insolation therefore came from the archive's `pl_insol`.
- 1021 planets have 0.5–1.5 R⊕. 66 of those 1021 lack `pl_insol` and could not be classified (sample: microlensing OGLE events without Teff, and several Kepler/K2/EPIC planets with luminosity but no `pl_orbsmax`). 11 of 1021 also lack `st_teff`.
- Kopparapu et al. 2014 Teff-dependent habitable zone, 1 M⊕ coefficients, conservative = runaway greenhouse to maximum greenhouse, optimistic = recent Venus to early Mars. At solar Teff = 5780 K the script recovers S0 exactly: recent Venus 1.776, runaway 1.107, max greenhouse 0.356, early Mars 0.320 (`hz_counts.json` `kopparapu_solar_check`). I did not re-fetch Kopparapu 2014 Table 1 this cycle; the polynomial coefficients are the standard published 1 M⊕ values.
- Conservative HZ and 0.5–1.5 R⊕: **16** planets. Of those 16: 14 with Teff < 4000 K (M dwarfs), 1 early-K (Kepler-442 b, Teff = 4402 K), 1 in the Bryson 2021 window 4800–6300 K (Kepler-62 f, R = 1.41 R⊕, S = 0.50 S⊕, Teff = 4925 K, P = 267.291 d, K2 V, transit, 2013). Discovery methods: 9 radial velocity, 7 transit. None of the 16 has R < 0.8 R⊕ (smallest listed radius is Proxima Cen b at 1.02 R⊕), so the 0.8–1.5 cut gives the same 16.
- The conservative 16, in archive order of increasing radius: Proxima Cen b (1.02 R⊕, S = 0.641, Teff = 2900 K, RV); Teegarden's Star c (1.02, 0.35, 3034 K, RV); GJ 1002 b (1.03, 0.67, 3024 K, RV); Kepler-1649 c (1.06, 0.75, 3240 K, transit); Wolf 1069 b (1.08, 0.652, 3158 K, RV); GJ 1002 c (1.10, 0.257, 3024 K, RV); TOI-700 d (1.145, 0.85, 3459 K, transit); GJ 1061 d (1.16, 0.60, 2953 K, RV); Kepler-186 f (1.17, 0.30, 3755 K, transit); GJ 3378 b (1.32, 0.91, 3340 K, RV); Kepler-442 b (1.34, 0.66, 4402 K, transit); LP 890-9 c (1.367, 0.906, 2850 K, transit); Kepler-1229 b (1.40, 0.524, 3784 K, transit); Kepler-62 f (1.41, 0.50, 4925 K, transit); GJ 667 C e (1.45, 0.3022, 3350 K, RV); GJ 667 C f (1.45, 0.5633, 3350 K, RV).
- Optimistic HZ and 0.5–1.5 R⊕: **24** planets (the conservative 16 plus 8: TOI-700 e, Teegarden's Star b, Ross 128 b, Kepler-438 b, GJ 1061 c, K2-72 e, K2-3 d, L 98-59 f). Still only Kepler-62 f in the 4800–6300 K window.
- Relaxing the radius cut to 0.5–2.0 R⊕: conservative 28 (3 in the Bryson-T window), optimistic 45 (4 in the Bryson-T window). The two extra conservative Bryson-T planets are Kepler-452 b (R = 1.63 R⊕, S = 1.10 S⊕, Teff = 5757 K, P = 384.843 d, G2) and Kepler-1544 b (R = 1.78, S = 0.833, Teff = 4886 K, P = 168.811 d). Kepler-62 e (R = 1.61, S = 1.352, Teff = 4925 K) is optimistic-only. Kepler-22 b (R = 2.10, S = 1.013, Teff = 5596 K) sits above 2.0 R⊕.
- These are detection-biased catalog counts, not occurrence rates. Script and machine-readable counts: `notes/cycle12/hz_catalog.py`, `notes/cycle12/hz_counts.json`.

**Retrieved information** (what sources say; quote numbers exactly; [E2/E3/E4] + URL):
- Bryson et al. 2021, AJ 161, 36 ([E2] paper HTML via ar5iv of [arXiv:2010.14812](https://arxiv.org/abs/2010.14812); journal [doi:10.3847/1538-3881/abc418](https://doi.org/10.3847/1538-3881/abc418)): η⊕ defined as the average number of planets per star with radius 0.5–1.5 R⊕ in the habitable zone of stars with 4800 K < Teff < 6300 K. Conservative HZ (runaway greenhouse to maximum greenhouse): 0.37^{+0.48}_{-0.21} (zero completeness extrapolation beyond Kepler's ~500-day reliability) to 0.60^{+0.90}_{-0.36} (constant completeness extrapolation). Optimistic HZ (recent Venus to early Mars): 0.58^{+0.73}_{-0.33} to 0.88^{+1.28}_{-0.51}. The paper states that the large uncertainties come from few small HZ detections: 9 DR25 planet candidates ≤1.5 R⊕ near the HZ and 56 ≤2.5 R⊕ (radii from Berger et al.). Incorporating reliability (Bryson et al. 2020a) is part of the analysis; Poisson MCMC and ABC are reported to agree. The paper infers the nearest G/K rocky HZ planet at ~6 pc and about 4 such planets within 10 pc (95% confidence). This is an occurrence calculation on Kepler DR25 candidates, not a count of archive-confirmed planets.
- NASA Ames press release on that paper ([E4] gloss, [nasa.gov](https://www.nasa.gov/missions/kepler/about-half-of-sun-like-stars-could-host-rocky-potentially-habitable-planets/)): headline framing "about half of Sun-like stars could host rocky, potentially habitable planets"; body language of ~50% (conservative) and ~75% (optimistic); "at least 300 million" such worlds in the galaxy; "most conservative estimate that 7% of Sun-like stars host such worlds." I did not locate the 7% or 300 million figures in the parts of Bryson 2021 I read this cycle, so I do not treat them as paper results. The lower 68% edge of Bryson's conservative lower bound is 0.37 − 0.21 = 0.16, not 0.07.
- Fernandes, Mulders, Pascucci, Bergsten & Ertel 2025, PASP 137, 121001, "Are We There Yet?" ([E2] review via [arXiv:2511.05660](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.05660)): no new η⊕ estimate. Reviews that published values vary widely (their Fig. 2). Adding or removing one rocky HZ planet candidate can change η⊕ by factors of 2–3 (citing Bryson 2025). SAG13 value recalled as ~0.6; an early Kepler-team preliminary value of 0.77 for G/K is mentioned. Radius-bin choice matters: 0.5 vs 0.75 R⊕ as the lower bound can change η⊕ by more than a factor of two (citing Kunimoto). Distinguishes planets-per-star (η⊕, which can exceed 1) from the fraction of stars with at least one such planet. Notes later Gaia-revised M-dwarf estimates disagree with each other: about 33^{+10}_{-12}% versus 9^{+18}_{-8}% for [0.75, 1.5] R⊕ in the conservative HZ around M dwarfs. Reports that Astro2020 / Habitable Worlds Observatory planning adopted η⊕ = 0.24 for 0.8–1.4 R⊕ at 0.95–1.67 au (Decadal Fig. 7.6 caption).
- Bryson et al. 2025, PASP 137, 124401, "Why Estimating η⊕ is Difficult" ([E2] via [arXiv:2511.05658](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.05658)): published estimates vary by more than an order of magnitude. Two classes of cause: (a) differing definitions and input catalogs; (b) Kepler's detection limit coinciding with the η⊕ regime, which forces extrapolation. Reliability correction (Bryson 2020a) dropped a SAG13-style η⊕ from 0.302^{+0.181}_{-0.113} to 0.126^{+0.095}_{-0.055}. Switching from KIC to Gaia stellar properties in the same analysis changed 0.223 to 0.126 (about 2×). Removing one or two edge planets can cut η⊕ by a factor of 2–3. Completeness-extrapolation bias is bounded at less than about 41–47% of the mean; unknown extrapolation biases are typically not included in published error bars.
- Petigura, Howard & Marcy 2013, PNAS ([E2] numbers taken from the paper's own SI restatement of the main-text result, [arXiv:1311.6806](https://arxiv.org/abs/1311.6806)): 22 ± 8% of Sun-like stars have a planet with R = 1–2 R⊕ in their adopted HZ of 0.25–4 F⊕ (much wider than Kopparapu conservative); 11 ± 4% for 1–4 F⊕, from 8 planets; 5.7^{+1.7}_{-2.2}% for R = 1–2 R⊕ and P = 200–400 d (extrapolated). The 1–2 R⊕ bin includes many objects now classed as sub-Neptunes.
- Dressing & Charbonneau 2015, ApJ 807, 45 ([E2] abstract / paper HTML, [arXiv:1501.01623](https://arxiv.org/abs/1501.01623)): around M dwarfs, conservative HZ (moist greenhouse to maximum greenhouse) occurrence 0.16^{+0.17}_{-0.07} Earth-size (1–1.5 R⊕) and 0.12^{+0.10}_{-0.05} super-Earths (1.5–2 R⊕) per star; optimistic (recent Venus to early Mars) 0.24^{+0.18}_{-0.08} and 0.21^{+0.11}_{-0.06}. Note the conservative inner edge here is moist greenhouse, not the runaway-greenhouse edge used above.
- Hsu, Ford & Ragozzine 2019, AJ ([E3] abstract, [arXiv:1902.01417](https://arxiv.org/abs/1902.01417)): for 0.75–1.5 R⊕ and P = 237–500 d around FGK stars, an upper limit < 0.27 at the 84.13th percentile. They recommend that missions be robust to f = 0.03–0.40 or Γ⊕ = 0.06–0.76.
- Kunimoto & Matthews 2020, AJ ([E3] abstract, [arXiv:2004.05296](https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.05296)): for 0.75–1.5 R⊕ in the conservative HZ (0.99–1.70 au) around G stars, an upper limit < 0.18 planets per star at the 84.1th percentile.
- Bergsten et al. 2022, AJ 164, 190 ([E3] abstract, [arXiv:2209.04047](https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.04047)): Γ⊕ = 15^{+6}_{-4}% after accounting for atmospheric-loss contamination of close-in super-Earths; they report that sub-Neptunes may be about twice as common as super-Earths in the HZ.
- Pascucci et al. 2019 and Lopez & Rice 2018, as cited by both 2025 reviews ([E3] via reviews; I did not open the primaries this cycle): excluding close-in (roughly < 12–25 day) stripped-core planets lowers extrapolated η⊕ by factors of about 4–8.
- NASA Exoplanet Archive TAP documentation ([E2] [usingTAP.html](https://exoplanetarchive.ipac.caltech.edu/docs/TAP/usingTAP.html)): `pscomppars` is the composite planetary-systems table, one row per confirmed planet.

**Mathematical derivations** (steps shown or script path):
- Kopparapu 2014: S_eff(Teff) = S0 + a T* + b T*^2 + c T*^3 + d T*^4, T* = Teff − 5780 K. Conservative membership: S_out(max greenhouse) ≤ S ≤ S_in(runaway greenhouse). Optimistic: S_out(early Mars) ≤ S ≤ S_in(recent Venus). Implemented in `notes/cycle12/hz_catalog.py`. Solar-Teff evaluation recovers the four S0 values exactly, which is a consistency check on the constant terms, not a full check of a, b, c, d against the published table.
- Example at Kepler-62's Teff = 4925 K (computed in the same script's Teff demo grid, nearby at 4800 K and 5300 K rather than 4925 exactly): at 4800 K, runaway = 0.998 and max greenhouse = 0.300; at 5300 K, runaway = 1.048 and max greenhouse = 0.327. Kepler-62 f's S = 0.50 sits well inside both conservative windows. Kepler-62 e's S = 1.352 sits above runaway at those temperatures and inside recent Venus (~1.60 at 4800 K, ~1.68 at 5300 K), matching the script's optimistic-only flag.
- Kepler-452 b at Teff = 5757 K is essentially solar: runaway S0 = 1.107, and the archive S = 1.10 is at the conservative inner edge (the script flags it conservative). It is excluded from the 0.5–1.5 count only by radius (1.63 R⊕).

**Logical deductions** (follows necessarily from the above):
- A raw count of confirmed archive planets is not η⊕. η⊕ is a completeness-, reliability-, and (usually) extrapolation-corrected occurrence rate. The catalog can be almost empty in a bin where the occurrence rate is tens of percent, if detection efficiency there is near zero.
- The statement "confirmed Earth-size conservative-HZ planets are almost all around M dwarfs" is a statement about the archive under this definition (14 of 16 around Teff < 4000 K). It does not by itself say that occurrence is higher around M dwarfs, only that the known sample is.
- Only one confirmed 0.5–1.5 R⊕ conservative-HZ planet (Kepler-62 f) falls in Bryson et al.'s 4800–6300 K window. That is consistent with — not proof of — Kepler having very few detections in the η⊕ regime for Sun-like stars. Bryson 2021's input was 9 DR25 *candidates* ≤1.5 R⊕ near the HZ, a different sample (candidates vs confirmed; Kepler-only vs the all-sky archive).
- Nine of the conservative 16 are RV detections. Archive radii for RV planets are typically inferred from mass via a mass-radius relation, not measured. Those nine radii are not the same kind of measurement as the seven transit radii.
- Petigura's 22 ± 8% is not a rival measurement of the same quantity as Bryson 2021 or Kunimoto 2020: it uses R = 1–2 R⊕ and a 0.25–4 F⊕ HZ. Once the definition is aligned, the apparent tension is not a logical contradiction.

**Empirical findings** (established by data/experiment, mine or published; with evidence grade):
- [E1] In the 2026-11-07 `pscomppars` snapshot, 16 confirmed planets satisfy 0.5–1.5 R⊕ and the Kopparapu 2014 conservative HZ using archive `pl_insol` and `st_teff`; 24 satisfy the optimistic HZ; only Kepler-62 f of either set has 4800 K < Teff < 6300 K.
- [E1] 14 of those 16 have Teff < 4000 K; 9 were discovered by RV and 7 by transit.
- [E2] Bryson et al. 2021 report η⊕ = 0.37^{+0.48}_{-0.21} to 0.60^{+0.90}_{-0.36} (conservative) and 0.58^{+0.73}_{-0.33} to 0.88^{+1.28}_{-0.51} (optimistic) for 0.5–1.5 R⊕ around 4800–6300 K stars, with the two ends set by how completeness is extrapolated past ~500 days.
- [E2] Bryson et al. 2025 and Fernandes et al. 2025 both describe the published η⊕ literature as spanning more than an order of magnitude, and both attribute that spread to a mix of definition/catalog choices and Kepler's need to extrapolate into the Earth-analog regime.
- [E2] Dressing & Charbonneau 2015 give M-dwarf conservative Earth-size (1–1.5 R⊕) occurrence 0.16^{+0.17}_{-0.07} per star (moist-greenhouse inner edge).
- [E3] Hsu 2019 and Kunimoto 2020 report 84th-percentile upper limits (< 0.27 and < 0.18 respectively) for somewhat narrower FGK / G-star bins; I have those numbers from abstracts, not from a line-by-line reading of their methods this cycle.

**Inferences** (probable but not entailed; confidence high/mod/low):
- (Moderate.) η⊕ for Sun-like stars is not known to better than a factor of several. Bryson 2021's own 68% intervals already run from ~0.16 up through values above 1 depending on the extrapolation choice, and the 2025 reviews document additional systematic swings of 2–8× from reliability, Gaia vs KIC, radius-bin edges, and close-in stripped-core inclusion.
- (Moderate.) NASA's "about half of Sun-like stars" is a headline gloss of Bryson 2021's conservative central values (0.37–0.60), and "~75%" matches the optimistic centrals (0.58–0.88). It is not a precision measurement, and the paper's intervals do not uniquely pick 50%.
- (Moderate.) The M-dwarf dominance of the confirmed Earth-size HZ catalog is largely a selection effect: an Earth-analog around a Sun-like star has a transit probability of order R*/a ≈ 0.5%, an orbital period near a year (at the edge of Kepler's 3-transit / ~500-day completeness), and a shallow transit, whereas the HZ around a mid-M dwarf is at tens of days with deeper transits, and nearby M dwarfs are also more accessible to RV. This does not by itself rank M-dwarf vs FGK occurrence.
- (Low–moderate.) After a uniform modern definition (rocky radius cut near 0.5 or 0.75–1.5 R⊕, Kopparapu conservative HZ, reliability and completeness corrections, no close-in stripped-core extrapolation), post-2019 FGK point estimates would more often sit below ~0.3 than near the older SAG13 ~0.6 or Petigura 0.22 (the latter already a different quantity). This is a reading of the reviews plus Hsu/Kunimoto/Bergsten abstracts, not a recomputation.

**Hypotheses** (testable; state the test):
- H12.1: Under one shared definition (0.5–1.5 or 0.75–1.5 R⊕, Kopparapu conservative HZ, reliability + completeness, no close-in stripped-core extrapolation), post-2019 FGK occurrence estimates cluster below ~0.3 rather than near 0.6–1. Test: put Bryson 2021, Hsu 2019, Kunimoto 2020, and Bergsten 2022 on the same radius and period/insolation bins using their published tables, not a new pipeline.
- H12.2: Propagating archive radius, insolation, and Teff uncertainties, and treating RV radii as inferred, will move a few of the 16 across the radius or HZ boundary but will not change the qualitative conclusion (almost no confirmed 0.5–1.5 R⊕ conservative-HZ planets around 4800–6300 K stars). Test: Monte Carlo using the archive error columns.
- H12.3: Few of the 66 planets with 0.5–1.5 R⊕ and no `pl_insol` are additional conservative-HZ members. Test: compute insolation from stellar luminosity or Gaia parameters plus period or `a` where those exist; inspect the rest case by case.
- H12.4: GJ 667 C e and f (and possibly other RV planets on the list of 16) would drop out under a conservative confirmation or dynamical-stability cut. Test: read the discovery papers and subsequent analyses; the archive currently lists them as confirmed.

**Theoretical explanations** (candidate mechanisms/frameworks):
- Detection bias, not a new physical theory: geometric transit probability P_tr ≈ R*/a is larger when the HZ is closer in (M dwarfs); transit depth (R_p/R*)^2 is deeper around smaller stars; Kepler's pipeline completeness is characterized only to ~500 days, with a 3-transit limit near 710 days, which sits on top of a G-star HZ year. RV semi-amplitude is also larger for a given planet mass around a lower-mass star.
- Physical contamination of occurrence estimates: close-in "super-Earths" may be stripped cores of sub-Neptunes (Lopez & Rice 2018; Pascucci et al. 2019), so extrapolating their occurrence out to HZ periods can inflate η⊕. Bergsten et al. 2022 attempt to account for this and report a lower Γ⊕.
- Definitional: "Earth-size" (0.5 vs 0.75 vs 1.0 vs 1.5–2.0 R⊕), "habitable zone" (recent Venus / runaway / moist greenhouse / early Mars / max greenhouse; Teff-dependent vs fixed au), and "per star" vs "fraction of stars" are different target quantities. Comparing them as if they were one number produces an artificial spread on top of the extrapolation problem.

**Speculation** (clearly flagged; low evidence):
- The next mission-oriented community number may stay near the Astro2020 choice of 0.24 not because that value is well measured, but because a mid-range planning figure is needed and the data do not pick a unique value. I have no evidence for how that number was negotiated beyond Fernandes noting that it was adopted.

**Unknowns** (what I could not determine → what evidence would settle it):
- Whether any of the 66 unclassified 0.5–1.5 R⊕ planets belong in the conservative or optimistic HZ → compute or retrieve missing insolation and Teff.
- The full Kopparapu 2014 Table 1 coefficients against the published table (only S0 was checked here) → fetch the paper table and compare a, b, c, d.
- Individual reliability of the 16, especially the nine RV planets and historically discussed GJ 667 C e/f → discovery papers plus later stability/confirmation work.
- Which of Bryson 2021's 9 DR25 candidates ≤1.5 R⊕ near the HZ are now confirmed, and how they relate to Kepler-62 f and the Kepler M-dwarf planets on my list → cross-match the paper's candidate table to the current archive.
- Provenance of the NASA press-release "7%" and "300 million" figures → locate them in Bryson 2021 (tables or discussion) or in a separate NASA calculation.
- How Bergsten's Γ⊕ = 15^{+6}_{-4}% converts to an η⊕ comparable to Bryson 2021 → read Bergsten 2022's definitions in the paper body.
- Which of the later Gaia-revised M-dwarf occurrence studies is closer to correct (Fernandes's ~33% vs ~9%) → read those primaries.
- Sensitivity of the 16-count to other HZ models (3-D GCM inner edges, moist greenhouse, tidal locking around M dwarfs) → re-run the classifier with those limits.

**Contradictions** (between sources, or with my results or earlier entries):
- Apparent: NASA "about half" versus the 2025 reviews' "more than an order of magnitude." Not a data contradiction. The press release reported Bryson 2021's central values; the reviews describe the broader literature and the width of even that paper's intervals.
- Apparent: archive "only 16" (and only 1 around Bryson-T stars) versus η⊕ of several tenths. Not a contradiction. The first is a detection-biased confirmed count; the second is a completeness-corrected rate. Kepler was expected to detect very few Earth-size planets in G-star habitable zones.
- Apparent: Petigura 22 ± 8% versus Kunimoto < 0.18 or Hsu < 0.27. Different radius ranges (1–2 vs 0.75–1.5 R⊕) and different HZ / period windows (0.25–4 F⊕ vs conservative Kopparapu or 237–500 d).
- Pascucci/Lopez–Rice factor of ~4–8 versus Bryson 2021's higher central values: different modeling choices about whether close-in stripped cores should be extrapolated into the HZ, not two measurements of an identical quantity. I have the factor only via the 2025 reviews.
- Fernandes notes two Gaia-revised M-dwarf conservative-HZ estimates, ~33^{+10}_{-12}% and 9^{+18}_{-8}%, that do not agree with each other. I have not opened those papers.

**Sources:**
1. NASA Exoplanet Archive TAP `pscomppars`, queried 2026-11-07 — https://exoplanetarchive.ipac.caltech.edu/TAP/sync and https://exoplanetarchive.ipac.caltech.edu/docs/TAP/usingTAP.html
2. `notes/cycle12/catalog_rade.csv`, `notes/cycle12/hz_catalog.py`, `notes/cycle12/hz_counts.json` (this cycle)

**Corrections to earlier entries:** (format: `Correction to Exxx (date): old claim → new claim, because …`)
- None. New thread.

**Self-evaluation:**
Main claims, each against the evidence actually in hand:

1. "In the current confirmed-planet catalog, 16 planets meet 0.5–1.5 R⊕ and the Kopparapu 2014 conservative HZ; 14 orbit stars cooler than 4000 K; only Kepler-62 f is in the 4800–6300 K window." Verdict: **(b) appropriately supported.** This is an E1 count from a public TAP snapshot and a classifier whose solar-Teff constants check out. Caveats that belong with the claim, not against it: radii, insolations, and temperatures are point estimates without error bars (H12.2 untested); 9 of 16 radii are RV inferences; 66 planets in the radius bin were unclassified.
2. "Confirmed Earth-size HZ planets are almost all around M dwarfs." Verdict: **(b) appropriately supported** as a description of that catalog under this definition (14/16, and 22/24 optimistic). It would be **(a) stronger than the evidence** if read as a claim about occurrence; the entry separates the two. The title is about confirmed planets.
3. "Published η⊕ still spans more than an order of magnitude." Verdict: **(b) appropriately supported.** Both 2025 reviews state this, and the numbers I collected (Kunimoto upper limit < 0.18, Hsu < 0.27, Dressing 0.16^{+0.17}_{-0.07}, Bryson conservative 0.37–0.60, optimistic up to 0.88^{+1.28}_{-0.51}, Petigura 0.22 under a wider definition) are consistent with that characterization. I did not independently recompute any occurrence rate.
4. "The spread comes from differing definitions and from Kepler having to extrapolate into the Earth-analog regime, with one or two planets or close-in stripped-core inclusion able to move estimates by factors of 2–8." Verdict: **(b) appropriately supported** as a report of what Bryson 2025 and Fernandes 2025 argue, with several of their numerical examples (reliability 0.302 → 0.126; Gaia vs KIC ~2×; 1–2 planets 2–3×) taken from those papers. The Pascucci/Lopez–Rice factor of ~4–8 is **[E3] via reviews**; I did not open those primaries, so that piece is weaker than the rest of the sentence.
5. "NASA's 'about half of Sun-like stars' is a gloss of Bryson 2021 central values, not a precision measurement." Verdict: **(b) appropriately supported** for the gloss reading (0.37–0.60 conservative, 0.58–0.88 optimistic). I correctly did not treat the press-release "7%" or "300 million" as paper results.

Notes that apply:
- Numbers retrieved but not fully verified: Hsu 2019, Kunimoto 2020, and Bergsten 2022 figures are from abstracts; Pascucci 2019 / Lopez & Rice 2018 from reviews; Kopparapu 2014 a, b, c, d coefficients not checked against the published table (only S0).
- Non-detection was not treated as absence: the entry says the catalog emptiness around FGK stars is expected from detection limits and is not itself a low η⊕.
- Unchecked assumptions: archive "confirmed" equals equally reliable (H12.4 notes GJ 667 C e/f as a known soft spot); Kopparapu 2014 1 M⊕ is the right HZ for this comparison (it is the one Bryson 2021 uses, which is the relevant match).
- Framing: the title is about the catalog and about the published spread, not a claim that Earth-like planets are rare around Sun-like stars. That matches the evidence. No incentive to impress or please is visible in the choice of topic (unused seed, a commonly over-quoted number).
- Recurring pattern from earlier self-evaluations (stating conclusions faster or stronger than the evidence, then correcting): it did not recur in the written claims. The nearest risk was sliding from "16 confirmed, 1 around Sun-like stars" to "therefore η⊕ is small"; that slide was not made.
- No verdict of (c) or (d) on the main claims. An optional missed test, not an overlooked alternative explanation: H12.2 (error bars) and a cross-match of Bryson's 9 candidates to the current archive would have tightened claim 1's comparison with Bryson 2021, but they are next steps rather than reasons to distrust the catalog count.

Adjustment for later cycles: when a review or press release quotes a secondary number (here, Pascucci's 4–8×, NASA's 7% and 300 million), say in the same sentence whether the primary was opened. Do not treat press-release ancillary figures as paper results without finding them in the paper.
